If this equation applies to our sliding book from earlier, we can see it still reduces to the familiar E equals MC squared, once the book eventually stops. So nothing new yet, but let's compare it to Einstein's full version. Every single term in his version had a mass, which means it only applies to matter. But what about light? Well, Einstein's version doesn't work. Minkowski's version does though. If we plug a zero in for mass, we ultimately get E equals PC. This is the energy of a photon, the particle of light. Minkowski version applies to matter and light, which is everything. Well, at least it was back then, but let's stay on topic. For Einstein and Minkowski, matter was the stuff that had mass. Light was the stuff that doesn't. And I think this is the real source of confusion around E equals MC squared. The terms “matter” and “mass” are often used interchangeably, but they're not the same thing. Matter is just stuff that has a property we've assigned to it called “mass.” Mass is not a physical thing. It's an abstract number. And light has a similar confusion. The terms “light” and “energy” are often used interchangeably, especially in particle physics, but they're also not the same thing. Light is just stuff that has a property we've assigned to it called “energy.” Energy is not a physical thing either. It's also an abstract number, a number we can use in our mathematics. See, math is not fundamental to the universe or anything. It's just a tool that physics uses to make predictions and interpret patterns in reality. And, as one of my old professors used to say, “physics is in the language.” When dealing with advanced physics like relativity or quantum mechanics, we have to be very careful with our language because we're discussing things in an unfamiliar realm. Say you've got an electron and a positron, a particle antiparticle pair. When they collide, they cease to exist and two photons are born. Did we just convert matter into energy via E equals MC squared? No! No, we did not! This is matter converting into light, not matter converting into energy. This light certainly has energy, but so do the electron and the positron. Those two also have a property called “mass” that the photons do not, but only because some of their energy happens to be confined. That's it. Matter and light are physical things we can observe. Mass and energy are just mathematical quantities. We made them up. But that doesn't mean they're not real. Lots of stuff is made up and real at the same time. Money is a perfect example. It's an abstract quantity that we've assigned to economic transactions. Measurements like mass and energy have a similar existence. First, we observe that something like matter has a behavior, like this sliding book slowing down. But we also notice that it doesn't stop instantaneously. The book is somehow resisting that slowdown. It's a behavior we call “inertia” and mass is a quantity we often assign to quantify inertia. Why do we do that? Because it's useful. That definition allows us to write equations like Newton's second law of motion. The friction on the book, divided by its mass, predicts a certain amount of slowing... and that prediction matches reality. Useful! Now let's look at energy. It happens to be the same before and after this reaction. It might have changed forms, mass and kinetic energy before and photon energy after, but the total amount never changes. Conservation of energy shall not be violated!! We've defined energy in such a way that it obeys that rule, which allows us to predict how energetic the resulting photons will be, among other things. Energy and mass are useful, and the way we define properties like that will determine what the equations look like. Rest energy might be E equals MC squared... if you measure energy in joules, mass in kilograms, and the speed of light in meters per second; but we don't have to use standard units like that. Remember, we made all this up like we made up money. We could just as easily measure all speeds as a multiple of the speed of light, in which case: C equals one and the equation is just E equals M. Or in English, rest energy is mass,... which, to be honest, is a pretty common thing to do in relativity. The point is, we decide how the measurements are defined and we decide what units are used. Those choices then determine what the equations look like. It's all made up! Math is not reality, but it has the power to tell us about reality, if we use it properly. And until next time, remember it's okay to be a little crazy. In 1922, the leading model for atoms was the Bohr model. Electrons orbiting around a dense clump of protons and probably something neutral we hadn't discovered yet. Anything going around in circles like those electrons has something called “angular momentum.” You can think of momentum as a kind of interaction currency. You know, anything you might assign an angle to, hence “angular.” For things like planets and stars, there are always smaller denominations of this momentum currency. It's like digital money where you can exchange fractions of pennies. But for electrons, this angular momentum is quantized, meaning it can only take on certain values. In other words, the denominations are fixed. You know, like paper money and coins. Only certain orbits are allowed inside an atom and nothing in between. This quantized model explained quite a bit about atoms, and so it wasn't really up for debate. What was up for debate was the orientation of these electron orbits. Could electrons orbit in any orientation they wanted or were the orientations also quantized? Stern and Gerlach devised an experiment to test just that. It all starts by placing a piece of silver foil in a really hot oven. After that, a slit filters those atoms into a beam. That beam of atoms is what they were running their tests on and they chose silver for three reasons: It was easy to acquire. It was easy to vaporize. And most importantly, the outer shell of the atom only had one electron. These shapes were important because one magnetic pole needed to be stronger than the other. If they were the same, nothing would happen. The debate was over what direction the atoms would deflect and by how much. We've got two possibilities: If electrons can orbit in any way they wish, then each atom in the beam will have a random orientation. The atoms more aligned with the magnets will deflect more, and the atoms less aligned will deflect less. The pattern on the glass plate should be a spread of silver atoms. However, if the orientations are quantized, then only certain orbital planes are allowed. The atoms in the beam should only deflect specific amounts,  forming distinct groups on the glass plate. In 1922, when this experiment was done, it seemed like a perfectly reasonable conclusion... to everyone, frankly. This outer electron doesn’t have angular momentum, like, at all. How can they get away with that nonsense? Welcome to quantum mechanics! And I don't mean “nonsense” like it's not a real thing. This is very real. I just hate it. Anyway, this is when physics started to reevaluate the Stern-Gerlach experiment. For four years now, they thought this experiment was showing orbital orientation, but that couldn't possibly be true anymore. If this electron has zero angular momentum, then so does the entire atom. No angular momentum means no magnetic effect, which means no deflection.  The Stern-Gerlach result should have looked like this: a single dot in the middle of the glass plate. This posed a problem because that's not what happened in the experiment. We know these atoms are deflected in a quantized way, and we know that it's due to magnetism. Your first thought might be that these particles have some kind of inherent magnetism, but that doesn't check out either. If they were just tiny magnets, they'd reorient to align with the field, and they'd all deflect the same direction. That's also not what happens. We need something that causes magnetism and doesn't allow for reorientation. Which brings us back to good old angular momentum. But if it's not orbital angular momentum, then what is it? Thankfully, in 1925, Uhlenbeck and Goudsmit had already suggested the answer: quantum spin. That came three years after the experiment, but one year before Schrodinger's upheaval, so they couldn't have suggested spin in response to this experiment. As far as these guys were concerned, the experiment was just fine. They were suggesting it for an entirely different reason. It just coincidentally also solved the Stern-Gerlach problem, once we realized there was one. But here's the craziest part of this story. Except we call the latitude “declination,” and we call the longitude “right ascension.” It lets us map out the locations of all the stars in the sky. Friedrich Bessel notice that Sirius’ right ascension was changing. Out of context, that wasn't all that surprising. Binary systems were already known to do this. Except Sirius wasn't a binary system. It was a single star. How could a star wobble all on its own? The most reasonable conclusion here is that it wasn't alone. And that's exactly what good old Friedrich thought. He was just a huge fan of binary systems, and loved to tell local astronomers when he found a new one. Don't let anyone tell you your hobbies are a waste of time, folks. The point is, there was finally photographic evidence that Sirius had a companion. What it didn't have was a name. But basically, an HR diagram is a plot of brightness against color. Brightness tells us energy output, and color tells us surface temperature. But that's not all. If we plot thousands of stars, we begin to see patterns. For example, the main sequence is all the stars in the first stage of their life, burning hydrogen. They're bright because they're hot or dim because they're cool. Stars in the later stages of their life clump together too, in even brighter bubbles. But because they're giant rather than hot. ## 38. This Paradox Took 17 Years To Solve. It's Still Debated.
**Channel:** The Science Asylum | **Views:** 434K | **Date:** 3 years ago | **Duration:** 11:33 | **ID:** PO4a2zO8zW8

the string does it snap if the acceleration is the same then the distance between them should also remain the same so the string should not snap right but if they accelerate anywhere near the speed of light length contraction happens so snap but the spaceships don't observe the length contraction and neither does the string so which is it does it snap or not we can solve this but it's going to test the limits of special relativity [Music] so which is it then snapped or not oh it definitely snaps how do you know this one took me longer to understand than i expected so buckle up crazies the problem we have here is called a paradox according to merriam-webster that's an argument that apparently derives self-contradictory conclusions by valid deductions from acceptable premises okay that's a lot of ten dollar words essentially if you start with ideas that you know to be true and through different trains of thought you can arrive at two opposing conclusions then you've got yourself a paradox now paradoxes aren't a problem with the universe they're a problem with us in other words one of our premises is faulty we either have a preconception we're unaware of or we've stepped beyond the scope of the model this spaceship paradox is no exception though it's actually one of the more recent paradoxes in special relativity to the timeline special relativity was published in 1905. general was 10 years later edmund dewan and michael baron wouldn't start this paradox until 1959. but as you can see here their solution wasn't very thorough this is the entire publication it did spark a lot of discussion though so dejuan revisited the topic in 1963 where he considered it in tandem with the famous pole barn paradox the debate centered around material stress and how that should be treated relativistically in 1976 john stewart bell of quantum entanglement fame finally fleshed out the nuances by adding a third observer to represent the previously abstract inertial reference frame we now refer to this as bell's spaceship paradox i'm not sure why it ended up named after him instead of dejuan and barron maybe because he was more famous i don't know anyway let's work out some of these nuances say i've made three clones arthur bernard and charles arthur and bernard's rockets are connected by a string exactly the length of the gap between them if both rockets begin accelerating at exactly the same moment at exactly the same rate then what happens to the string to be clear all observers should agree on whether or not the string snaps observers can disagree about when events occur how far apart they are and even sometimes their order but they must all agree on which events occur since charles is stationary he would observe relativistic length contraction the rockets contract in length but the string can't because it's attached to the rockets therefore the string snaps under the stress so paradox solved bingo bango not so fast we haven't considered the other two observers yet from the point of view of arthur and bernard the string should be stationary that means no length contraction which means no snapping of the string and there's our contradiction our paradox the string can't be both broken and unbroken all observers must agree on which events occur the string either snaps for everyone or it doesn't for everyone charles represents what we call an inertial frame of reference for special relativity inertial just means stationary or moving steadily in a space-time diagram those are always straight paths this means we can trust our conclusions about what charles sees he observes length contraction so the string snaps i suppose you could stop there it's the only obvious inertial reference frame in this scenario and we know all observers must agree on which events occur so we know arthur and bernard will also see the string snap but where's the fun in that besides this is the science asylum if we're going to call this paradox solved i think our aspiration should be a bit higher than sufficient we need to ask a deeper question it may be true that the string also breaks for both arthur and bernard but why they don't observe the length contraction so what's the cause of the snap well as i said before there are two possibilities we either have a preconception we're unaware of or we've stepped beyond the scope of the model it's the second one isn't it huh especial relativity can't handle accelerations right actually no that that's a common misconception special relativity can handle acceleration just fine the equations don't look particularly elegant but they exist and they're valid it's accelerated frames of reference that are problematic but even those are workable if we're sneaky about it in a space-time diagram accelerated paths are hyperbolas because flat space-time is hyperbolic switching between inertial frames of reference is easy you just rotate the axis to shift to an accelerated frame of reference you'd have to curve the axis which officially jumps out of special and into general relativity we don't want to do that if we don't have to general relativity is nasty what we're going to do instead is imagine this hyperbola is just a bunch of little straight paths if there are enough of those tiny segments we can't tell the difference it's mathematically equivalent to the hyperbola now if that sounds too much like math hand waving to you there is a more tangible way to visualize this imagine there's a big set of inertial pilots all watching an accelerated rocket each of these new ships will be traveling at a steady speed in a straight line no acceleration they're just positioned in such a way that they're going the exact same speed as the accelerating ship when that accelerating ship catches up with them we call these new reference frames momentarily co-moving inertial frames or mcifs they're momentarily in the same frame of reference as the accelerating ship it's the same as splitting the hyperbola into a bunch of tiny straight segments each segment being one of those mcifs the point is special relativity can handle accelerated reference frames just fine if you're careful there's no need to invoke general relativity we haven't stepped beyond the scope of our model so we must have a hidden preconception what preconceived notion do we have it has to do with the distance between the rockets it changes it changes when we first described the scenario we said the rockets must begin accelerating at the same moment and must have the same acceleration rate that's the problem there's only one observer that measures those rates to be the same charles for him the space-time paths or world lines for arthur and bernard look like this since they accelerate at the same rate their paths have the same shape the rockets maintain a steady distance from each other while contracting in length but the string is attached to both ships so it can't contract even though it's trying to the extra tension makes it snap it also snaps for arthur and bernard but for an entirely different reason because their rockets move apart you always measure length or distance parallel with your own space axis hence these distance measurements charles made from his own frame of reference but bernard's axes change over time if his space axis changes then how he measures distance also changes bernard sees arthur accelerate too quickly which pulls on the string making it snap arthur sees something similar in his frame bernard is lagging behind he's break oh now we're cooking with gas [Laughter] yet another assumption we've made here is that the string is incredibly weak it has no effect on how the rockets move technically this string breaks is so intense that the mass of the star noticeably decreases it's called the eddington limit if a star tries to form with a higher mass the mass of that star will decrease down to that limit we enough for that process to reach a maximum of about 200 solar masses this estimate is consistent with data from our gravitational wave observatories the vast majority are actually under 100 black
